Ziprasidone: (Major) Ziprasidone is partially metabolized via the hepatic CYP3A4 isoenzyme system. Decreased metabolism of ziprasidone may lead to clinically important side effects. Drugs having the potential to decrease the elimination of ziprasidone via inhibition of CYP3A4 include nefazodone.
ZOLMitriptan: (Major) Medications that augment release, decrease the metabolism of, or decrease the reuptake of serotonin, like the serotonin-receptor agonists, may interact with nefazodone by causing serotonin-related side effects, including serotonin syndrome. Whenever possible, these drug combinations should be avoided. If serotonin-syndrome is suspected, offending agents should be discontinued.
Zolpidem: (Moderate) Consider decreasing the dose of zolpidem if coadministration with nefazodone is necessary. Zolpidem is a CYP3A4 substrate and nefazodone is a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor. Coadministration with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors increased the AUC of zolpidem by 34% to 70%.
Zuranolone: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of zuranolone and nefazodone. Concomitant use may increase zuranolone exposure and may also increase the risk for additive CNS depression. If concomitant use is necessary, decrease the zuranolone dose to 30 mg once daily and monitor for zuranolone-related adverse effects. Zuranolone is a CYP3A substrate, nefazodone is a strong CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with CNS depression. Concomitant use with another strong CYP3A inhibitor increased zuranolone overall exposure by 1.62-fold.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  Roughly 16% of patients taking nefazodone may discontinue treatment due to an adverse experience. The most common side effects reported by patients treated with nefazodone in clinical trials versus placebo include: xerostomia (25% vs. 13%), drowsiness (25% vs. 14%), nausea/vomiting (22%/2% vs. 12%/1%), dizziness (17% vs. 5%), and constipation (14% vs. 8%). Asthenia (11% vs. 5%), lightheadedness (10% vs. 3%), and confusion (7% vs. 2%) were also common. In adults receiving nefazodone doses = 20 mmHg change from baseline) occurs in roughly 2—3% of patients on nefazodone.Changes in sexual function are not frequent with nefazodone. Nefazodone-related sexual dysfunction may include libido decrease (1%), abnormal ejaculation (0.2%) and anorgasmia (0.1%). Impotence (erectile dysfunction) was reported as frequent (1% of patients) in tabulated data from all premarketing trials (uncontrolled and controlled studies), although this side effect was not necessarily caused by nefazodone. There have been rare post-market reports of priapism associated with nefazodone. If priapism occurs, therapy should be immediately discontinued and a qualified health care professional consulted. A urologist consultation may be needed.Events in clinical trials that had an incidence of >= 5% with nefazodone and occurred at a frequency greater than with placebo: headache (36% vs. 33%); infection (8% vs. 6%); dyspepsia (9% vs. 7%); diarrhea (8% vs. 7%); insomnia (11% vs. 9%); appetite stimulation (5% vs. 3%); and pharyngitis (6% vs. 5%). Nefazodone may be less likely than the SSRIs to induce insomnia; some studies have shown little effect of nefazodone on sleep architecture or REM sleep. Roughly 1.5% of patients who discontinue nefazodone do so because of complaints of insomnia.Other adverse events with an incidence of 1—5% for nefazodone, but that occurred at a slightly higher rate than with placebo include the following, as listed by organ system. Body as a whole: flu-like symptoms, fever and chills, neck rigidity. Dermatological: pruritus and rash (unspecified). Metabolic events: peripheral edema, polydipsia (thirst). Musculoskeletal: arthralgia. Nervous System: ataxia, abnormal dreams, flushing (vasodilation), hypertonia, decreased concentration or memory impairment (impaired cognition), incoordination, paresthesias, psychomotor retardation, tremor. Emotional lability, anxiety, agitation, and dysphoria are rare and do not occur more frequently than with placebo. Respiratory: increased cough. Special senses: dysgeusia, tinnitus. Urogenital: increased urinary frequency, urinary tract infection, urinary retention, vaginitis and mastalgia.Frequent events not previously mentioned, but reported by 1% of patients in tabulated data from all premarketing trials (uncontrolled and controlled studies) included gastroenteritis, dyspnea, and bronchitis; however, such events, though reported with nefazodone, were not necessarily caused by the drug.Premarketing studies of nefazodone found infrequent elevations in hepatic enzymes. However, there have been several published post-market reports of hepatocellular injury and hepatitis in association with nefazodone, some of which have resulted in hepatic failure, transplantation, or death. In published reported cases, patients developed nausea, vomiting, elevated hepatic enzymes (AST and ALT in the thousands), elevated prothrombin times and INRs, fatigue, and jaundice within 14—28 weeks of the initiation of nefazodone; hepatic encephalopathy developed within 4—6 weeks of the onset of jaundice. Histologically, all liver biopsies showed hepatic necrosis in the centrolobular areas (zone 3 of the hepatic acinus). The authors of these case reports have suggested the need for baseline liver chemistries prior to the initiation of nefazodone and periodic monitoring thereafter. The reported rate in the United States is about 1 case of hepatic failure resulting in death or transplant per 250,000—300,000 patient-years of nefazodone treatment. The total patient-years is a summation of each patient's duration of exposure expressed in years. For example, 1 patient-year is equal to 2 patients each treated for 6 months, 3 patients each treated for 4 months, etc. This represents a rate of about 3—4 times the estimated background rate of liver failure. This rate is an underestimate because of under reporting of adverse drug reactions in general, and the true risk could be considerably greater than this. A large cohort study of antidepressant users found no cases of liver failure leading to death or transplant among nefazodone users in about 30,000 patient-years of exposure. The spontaneous report data and the cohort study results provide estimates of the upper and lower limits of the risk of liver failure in nefazodone treated patients, but are not capable of providing a precise risk estimate. The time to liver injury for the reported liver failure cases resulting in death or transplant generally ranged from 2 weeks to 6 months on nefazodone therapy. Although some reports described dark urine and nonspecific prodromal symptoms (e.g., anorexia, malaise, and GI symptoms), other reports did not describe the onset of clear prodromal symptoms prior to the onset of jaundice. Patients who develop evidence of hepatocellular injury (e.g., increased serum AST or serum ALT levels >3 times the upper limit of normal) should be withdrawn from the drug. These patients should be presumed to be at increased risk for liver injury if nefazodone is reintroduced and accordingly, such patients should not be considered for retreatment.Other postmarketing reports of adverse experiences are similar to those seen during the premarketing evaluation of nefazodone. Voluntary reports of ADRs temporally associated with nefazodone received since market introduction not listed above and for which a causal relationship has not been established include: anaphylactoid reactions; angioedema; convulsions (including grand mal seizures); galactorrhea; gynecomastia (male); hyperprolactinemia; hyponatremia; rhabdomyolysis (when taken in conjunction with the HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors); serotonin syndrome; Stevens-Johnson syndrome; and thrombocytopenia.A neonatal abstinence syndrome has been reported in infants exposed to serotonergic agents in utero. After birth, symptoms consistent with withdrawal (i.e., poor feeding, hypoglycemia, hypothermia, lethargy or irritability, vomiting, etc.) were noted. Such complications can arise immediately upon delivery. Other symptoms have included respiratory distress, cyanosis, apnea, seizures, temperature instability, feeding difficulty, hypotonia, hypertonia, hyperreflexia, tremor, jitteriness, and constant crying. Serum concentrations of the serotonergic agent were measurable in the infants affected. Several other symptoms (bloody stools, necrotizing enterocolitis) may have been attributable to rebound platelet activation on withdrawal of the exposure to the SSRI. Neonatal symptoms generally improved over several days. Additionally, a cohort study of 55 women revealed that 22% (12/55) of neonates exposed to an SSRI in the third trimester had complications requiring treatment or extended hospitalization compared with 6% in comparison groups. Complications included respiratory distress (n=9), hypoglycemia (n=2) and jaundice (n=1). The incidence of prematurity in the third trimester SSRI group was significant at 20% vs. 3.7% of controls. These features are consistent with either a direct toxic effect of serotonergic agents, or, possibly a drug discontinuation syndrome. It should be noted that, in some cases, the clinical picture is consistent with serotonin syndrome. When treating a pregnant woman with an SSRI or other serotonergic agent during the third trimester, the physician should carefully consider the potential risks and benefits of treatment. If clinically feasible, and taking the drug half-life into consideration, tapering of the serotonergic agent prior to delivery may be considered as an alternative.All effective antidepressants can precipitate mania in predisposed individuals suffering from depression. If mania occurs, the antidepressant should be held and appropriate therapy to treat the manic symptoms initiated. Suicidal ideation has been reported in antidepressant clinical trials. The symptoms of aggressiveness, akathisia (psychomotor restlessness), agitation, anxiety, insomnia, irritability, hostility, mania, hypomania, impulsivity, and panic attacks have been reported in adult and pediatric patients being treated with antidepressants for major depressive disorder as well as for other indications, both psychiatric and nonpsychiatric. Although a causal link between the emergence of such symptoms and either the worsening of depression and/or the emergence of suicidal impulses has not been established, there is concern that such symptoms may represent precursors to emerging suicidality. Consideration should be given to changing the therapeutic regimen, including possibly discontinuing the medication, in patients whose depression is persistently worse, or who are experiencing emergent suicidality or symptoms that might be precursors to worsening depression or suicidal ideation. A change or discontinuation of the therapeutic regimen should especially be considered if these symptoms are severe, abrupt in onset, or were not part of the patient's presenting symptoms. Many of these adverse symptoms have been reported in clinical trials and have not always been associated with suicidality. Rapid discontinuation of SSRIs may also result in many of these adverse CNS events.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    ataxia, dizziness, drowsiness, dysarthria, flushing, headache, hyperesthesia, hyperkinesis, hypersalivation, hypertonia, hypotonia, insomnia, memory impairment, myoclonia, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, neuropathic pain, paresthesias, psychomotor impairment, ptosis, seizures, serotonin syndrome, stroke, tremor, vertigo
    General Information
    During clinical trials, the following centrally-mediated (CNS) effects occurred in at least 1% of patients receiving nefazodone and more frequently than in patients receiving placebo: headache (36% vs 33%), drowsiness (25% vs 14%), dizziness (17% vs 5%), insomnia (11% vs 9%), lightheadedness (10% vs 3%), memory impairment (4% vs 2%), paresthesias (4% vs 2%), vasodilation/flushing (4% vs 2%), abnormal dreams (3% vs 2%), decreased concentration (3% vs 1%), ataxia (2% vs 0%), incoordination (2% vs 1%), psychomotor impairment (2% vs 1%), tremor (2% vs 1%), and hypertonia (1% vs 0%). Drowsiness and dizziness were considered dose-related. Dizziness and insomnia were the causes of treatment discontinuation in 1.9% and 1.5% of study patients, respectively. Other CNS effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of nefazodone included vertigo, twitching, apathy, abnormal gait, abnormal thinking, decreased attention, neuropathic pain, dysarthria, and myoclonia. Rare events (< 0.1%) included hyperkinesis, hypersalivation, stroke, hyperesthesia, hypotonia, ptosis, and neuroleptic malignant syndrome. During post-market use, serotonin syndrome and seizures (including grand mal seizures) have been reported. Due to the high incidence of sedation associated with nefazodone, patients should be cautioned against driving, operating machinery, or performing other tasks requiring mental alertness until they know how the drug affects them.

    Headline
    blurred vision, conjunctivitis, diplopia, dysgeusia, hearing loss, hyperacusis, keratoconjunctivitis, mydriasis, night blindness, ocular pain, otalgia, photophobia, scotomata, tinnitus, visual impairment, xerophthalmia

Scotomata and visual trails were not observed at doses below 300 mg/day during clinical trials; however, there have been post-marketing reports of such events at doses below 300 mg/day. Rare events (< 0.1%) included deafness (hearing loss), glaucoma, night blindness, and taste loss (ageusia).

    Headline
    angina, AV block, bradycardia, heart failure, hypertension, hypotension, orthostatic hypotension, pallor, sinus tachycardia, syncope
    General Information
    Blockade of alpha-1 adrenergic receptors by nefazodone can produce cardiovascular effects such as hypotension, reflex tachycardia, and minor changes in ECG patterns. During clinical trials, the following adverse cardiovascular effects occurred in at least 1% of patients receiving nefazodone and more frequently than in patients receiving placebo: hypotension (2% vs 1%) and orthostatic hypotension (4% vs 1%). Pooled data from clinical trials showed a statistically significant difference between nefazodone and placebo for sinus bradycardia (1.5% vs 0.4%), although there were no obvious clinical differences. Other cardiac effects or related symptoms reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of nefazodone included sinus tachycardia, hypertension, syncope, ventricular extrasystoles, and angina pectoris. Rare events (< 0.1%) included AV block, congestive heart failure, hemorrhage, pallor, and varicose veins.

    Headline
    ejaculation dysfunction, galactorrhea, gynecomastia, hyperprolactinemia, impotence (erectile dysfunction), libido decrease, libido increase, orgasm dysfunction, priapism
    General Information
    Unlike many other antidepressants, changes in sexual function are not frequent with nefazodone. During clinical trials, libido decrease occurred in 1% of patients receiving nefazodone and less than 1% of patients receiving placebo. Other pre-marketing reports show that impotence (erectile dysfunction) (>= 1%), libido increase (0.1—1%) ejaculation dysfunction (0.1—1%), and orgasm dysfunction (anorgasmia < 0.1%) have also occurred. Increased prolactin (hyperprolactinemia), galactorrhea, and gynecomastia (in males) have been reported during post-market use. Some endocrine effects (e.g., galactorrhea, gynecomastia) and changes in sexual functioning may be due to elevated prolactin levels. There have been rare post-market reports of priapism associated with nefazodone. If priapism occurs, therapy should be immediately discontinued and a qualified health care professional consulted. A urologist consultation may be needed.

Although weight loss was reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-marketing studies, no difference was observed between nefazodone and placebo in the proportion of patients with a weight change of at least 7%.

    Headline
    elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic encephalopathy, hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatitis, hepatotoxicity, jaundice
    General Information
    Cases of life-threatening hepatotoxicity, including hepatic failure, have been reported in patients treated with nefazodone. Premarketing studies of nefazodone found infrequent elevations in hepatic enzymes, and rarely hepatitis. However, there have been several published postmarket reports of hepatocellular injury and hepatitis in association with nefazodone, some of which have resulted in hepatic failure, transplantation, or death. In published reported cases, patients developed nausea, vomiting, elevated hepatic enzymes (AST and ALT in the thousands), elevated prothrombin times and INRs, fatigue, and jaundice within 14 to 28 weeks of the initiation of nefazodone; hepatic encephalopathy developed within 4 to 6 weeks of the onset of jaundice. Histologically, all liver biopsies showed hepatic necrosis in the centrolobular areas (zone 3 of the hepatic acinus). The authors of these case reports have suggested the need for baseline liver function tests (LFTs) prior to the initiation of nefazodone and periodic LFT monitoring thereafter. The reported rate in the United States is about 1 case of hepatic failure resulting in death or transplant per 250,000 to 300,000 patient-years of nefazodone treatment. The total patient-years is a summation of each patient's duration of exposure expressed in years. For example, 1 patient-year is equal to 2 patients each treated for 6 months, 3 patients each treated for 4 months, etc. This represents a rate of about 3 to 4-times the estimated background rate of liver failure. This rate is an underestimate because of underreporting of adverse drug reactions in general, and the true risk could be considerably greater than this. A large cohort study of antidepressant users found no cases of liver failure leading to death or transplant among nefazodone users in about 30,000 patient-years of exposure. The spontaneous report data and the cohort study results provide estimates of the upper and lower limits of the risk of liver failure in nefazodone treated patients, but are not capable of providing a precise risk estimate. The time to liver injury for the reported liver failure cases resulting in death or transplant generally ranged from 2 weeks to 6 months on nefazodone therapy. Although some reports described dark urine and nonspecific prodromal symptoms (e.g., anorexia, general feelings of sickness, and GI symptoms), other reports did not describe the onset of clear prodromal symptoms prior to the onset of jaundice. Patients who develop evidence of hepatocellular injury (e.g., increased serum AST or serum ALT levels of at least 3 times the upper limit of normal) or clinical signs and symptoms of liver injury (e.g., jaundice) should be withdrawn from the drug. These patients should be presumed to be at increased risk for liver injury if nefazodone is reintroduced and accordingly, do not reintroduce nefazodone.

    General Information

During post-market use, Stevens-Johnson syndrome has occurred; however causality to the drug has not been established. Allergic reactions (unspecified) were reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-market evaluations. Anaphylactoid reactions and angioedema have been reported during post-market experience.

    Headline
    agitation, confusion, depression, euphoria, hallucinations, hostility, mania, paranoia, suicidal ideation
    General Information
    During clinical trials, confusion occurred in 7% of patients receiving nefazodone and was considered a dose-related effect. Psychiatric effects reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients included depersonalization, hallucinations, euphoria, hostility, derealization, paranoia, and suicidal ideation (i.e., suicide attempt, suicidal thoughts, suicide). Agitation was the cause of treatment discontinuation in 1.2% of study patients. Mania can occur in predisposed patients during treatment with an antidepressant. Monitor all antidepressant-treated patients for any indication for worsening of depression or the condition being treated and the emergence of suicidal behaviors or suicidal ideation, especially during the initial few months of drug therapy and after dosage changes. In a pooled analysis of placebo-controlled trials of antidepressants (n = 4,500 pediatrics and 77,000 adults), there was an increased risk for suicidal thoughts and behaviors in patients 24 years of age and younger receiving an antidepressant versus placebo, with considerable variation in the risk of suicidality among drugs. The difference in the absolute risk of suicidal thoughts and behaviors across different indications was highest in those with major depression. No suicides occurred in any of the pediatric trials. These studies did not show an increase in the risk of suicidal thoughts and behavior with antidepressant use in patients over 24 years of age; there was a reduction in risk with antidepressant use in patients aged 65 and older. Caregivers and/or patients should immediately notify the prescriber of changes in behavior or suicidal ideation during nefazodone treatment.

    Headline
    arthralgia, arthropathy, rhabdomyolysis
    General Information
    During clinical trials, arthralgia was reported in 1% of patients receiving nefazodone and less than 1% of patients receiving placebo. Other musculoskeletal effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of nefazodone included arthropathy (i.e., arthritis), tenosynovitis, muscle stiffness, and bursitis. Tendinous contracture was reported rarely (< 0.1%). Rhabdomyolysis has been reported during post-market use in some patients receiving a combination of nefazodone and lovastatin or simvastatin; however, a causal relationship to nefazodone has not been established.

    Headline
    dehydration, gout, hypercholesterolemia, hypoglycemia, hyponatremia, peripheral edema, polydipsia
    General Information
    During clinical trials, the following metabolic effects occurred in at least 1% of patients receiving nefazodone and more frequently than in patients receiving placebo: peripheral edema (3% vs 2%) and thirst/polydipsia (1% vs < 1%). Metabolic or nutritional effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of nefazodone included gout, dehydration, and increased lactic dehydrogenase. Rare events (< 0.1%) included hypercholesterolemia and hypoglycemia. Hyponatremia has been reported during post-market use. Although not reported by the manufacturer of nefazodone, hyponatremia due to the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone (SIADH) has occurred during the use of some other antidepressants (e.g., SSRIs).Cases with serum sodium lower than 110 mmol/L have been reported.

    Headline
    asthenia, chills, edema, fever, infection, influenza, malaise, pelvic pain
    General Information
    During clinical trials, the following general adverse effects occurred in at least 1% of patients receiving nefazodone and more frequently than in patients receiving placebo: asthenia (11% vs 5%), infection (8% vs 6%), influenza (3% vs 2%), chills (2% vs 1%), fever (2% vs 1%), and neck rigidity (1% vs 0%). Asthenia was the cause of treatment discontinuation in 1.3% of study patients. Other general effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of nefazodone included malaise, facial edema, hang-over effect, enlarged abdomen, hernia, and pelvic pain. Cellulitis was reported rarely (< 0.1%).

    Headline
    neonatal abstinence syndrome
    General Information
    A neonatal abstinence syndrome has been reported in infants exposed to serotonergic agents, like nefazodone, in utero. After birth, symptoms consistent with withdrawal (i.e., poor feeding, hypoglycemia, hypothermia, lethargy or irritability, vomiting, etc.) were noted. Such complications can arise immediately upon delivery. Other symptoms have included respiratory distress, cyanosis, apnea, seizures, temperature instability, feeding difficulty, hypotonia, hypertonia, hyperreflexia, tremor, jitteriness, and constant crying. Serum concentrations of the serotonergic agent were measurable in the infants affected. Several other symptoms (bloody stools, necrotizing enterocolitis) may have been attributable to rebound platelet activation on withdrawal of the exposure to the SSRI. Neonatal symptoms generally improved over several days. A cohort study of 55 women revealed that 22% (12/55) of neonates exposed to an SSRI in the third trimester had complications requiring treatment or extended hospitalization compared with 6% in comparison groups. Complications included respiratory distress (n=9), hypoglycemia (n=2) and jaundice (n=1). The incidence of prematurity in the third trimester SSRI group was significant at 20% vs. 3.7% of controls. These features are consistent with either a direct toxic effect of serotonergic agents, or, possibly a drug discontinuation syndrome. It should be noted that, in some cases, the clinical picture is consistent with serotonin syndrome. When treating a pregnant woman with an SSRI or other serotonergic agent during the third trimester, the physician should carefully consider the potential risks and benefits of treatment. If clinically feasible, and taking the drug half-life into consideration, tapering of the serotonergic agent prior to delivery may be considered as an alternative.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      neonatal abstinence syndrome
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    withdrawal
    General Information
    Gradual tapering is advisable during discontinuation of antidepressants to minimize the occurrence of potential discontinuation symptoms. Frequent symptoms which have occurred with serotonin reuptake inhibitors include dizziness, vertigo, nausea, vomiting, flu-like symptoms, sensory disturbances (e.g., paresthesias, electric shock sensation), sleep disturbances, irritability, anxiety, and/or agitation. Antidepressants with short half-lives generally have an increased frequency and severity of symptoms. Two days after abrupt discontinuation of nefazodone, one patient experienced asthenia, fatigue, difficulty in maintaining sleep due to vivid nightmares, nocturnal twitching, muscle pains primarily located in legs, severe headache, nausea, vomiting, and anxiety combined with agitation. Five days after cessation of the drug, all symptoms resolved spontaneously.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      withdrawal
      Severity
      Moderate

Description
  Text
  Nefazodone is an oral antidepressant with a mechanism of action distinct from other antidepressants. Chemically, it is a synthetically derived phenylpiperazine. Nefazodone is structurally similar to trazodone; however, nefazodone causes less sedation or orthostatic hypotension. Nefazodone is approved for the treatment of major depression in adults. Nefazodone lacks the potential for cardiovascular toxicity seen with tricyclic antidepressants. Insomnia and inhibition of REM sleep, which are frequently associated with the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, are not associated with nefazodone. Nefazodone's product label contains a boxed warning of rare cases of hepatic failure leading to transplantation or death; due to these risks, nefazodone is not often a first choice for antidepressant treatment. In addition, all product labels for antidepressants contain a boxed warning related to an increased risk of suicidality in children, adolescents, and young adults during the initial stages of therapy when treating depression or other conditions; therefore, the necessity of pharmacologic therapy versus the potential risks should be carefully considered in these populations.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Mechanism of Action: The pharmacologic actions of nefazodone involve both the serotonergic and, to a lesser extent, the noradrenergic systems. Within the serotonergic system, nefazodone is a potent antagonist at type 2 serotonin (5-HT2) post-synaptic receptors. Nefazodone also inhibits pre-synaptic serotonin (5-HT) reuptake (similar to fluoxetine-type antidepressants) but this mechansim is secondary in importance. Both mechanisms increase the amount of serotonin available to interact with 5-HT receptors. Within the noradrenergic system, nefazodone inhibits norepinephrine uptake minimally and this effect is lost with chronic dosing. In addition, nefazodone has been shown to antagonize alpha1-adrenergic receptors. Blockade of alpha1-adrenergic receptors produces sedation, muscle relaxation, and cardiovascular effects such as hypotension, reflex tachycardia, and minor changes in ECG patterns. Nefazodone has no significant affinity for benzodiazepine, cholinergic, dopaminergic, histaminic, or beta or alpha2-adrenergic receptors. As with other antidepressants, nefazodone's antidepressant effects may not be noticeable for several weeks. Nefazodone does not inhibit REM sleep.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Following oral administration, nefazodone is rapidly and completely absorbed. Absolute bioavailability is only 20% due to extensive first-pass metabolism. Food delays the absorption of nefazodone and further decreases bioavailability by roughly 20%. Peak plasma concentrations of nefazodone occur in 1 hour; with steady-state concentrations achieved in about 4—5 days of twice daily dosing.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Patients with renal impairment (i.e., CrCl 7—60 ml/min/1.73m2) exhibit similar steady-state nefazodone concentrations to those patients with normal renal function. No information is available for anuric patients; nefazodone is not expected to be removed by hemodialysis.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    In elderly patients, both Cpmax and AUC are twice as high compared to younger male patients after single doses; however, after multiple daily dosing the steady-state concentrations observed are only 10—20% higher.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    In female patients, both Cpmax and AUC are twice as high compared to younger male patients after single doses; however, after multiple daily dosing the steady-state concentrations observed are only 10—20% higher.
  Text
  Nefazodone is administered orally. Nefazodone is distributed in most body tissues including the central nervous system. Protein binding is approximately 99% and metabolism occurs by n-dealkylation and aliphatic and aromatic hydroxylation via the hepatic microsomal CYP3A4 isoenzyme. At least 2 major active metabolites have been identified: hydroxynefazodone and triazoledione. Hydroxynefazodone has the same pharmacologic profile as the parent drug whereas the triazoledione metabolite has one-seventh the activity of nefazodone at 5HT-2A receptors and no serotonin reuptake activity. One minor metabolite, mCPP, also exists and has nonselective serotonin agonist activity at 5HT-1A, 5HT-1C, and 5HT-3 receptors. Nefazodone's half life ranges from 2 to 4 hours. The half-lives of the metabolites are 1.5—4 hours for hydroxynefazodone, 4 to 8 hours for mCPP, and 18 hours for triazoledione. Excretion of nefazodone and its metabolites occur through both the urine (55%) and feces (20 to 30%).
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) enzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4, CYP1A2, CYP2D6, CYP2C
In vitro data indicate that CYP3A4 is primarily responsible for the metabolism of nefazodone and its metabolites hydroxynefazodone and triazoledione, while the metabolite mCPP is primarily metabolized by CYP2D6. Nefazodone is a potent inhibitor of CYP3A4 and a weak inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2D6, and CYP2C. The metabolite mCPP is a weak inhibitor of CYP2D6.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Generally not recommended in hepatic disease. Dosage should be modified depending on clinical response and degree of hepatic impairment, but no quantitative recommendations are available. Elimination is reduced in patients with cirrhosis. Withdraw drug if evidence of hepatocellular injury such as active liver disease or increased LFT levels >3 times the upper limit of normal occurs (see Contraindications).
  Renal Impairment
  Specific guidelines for dosage adjustments in renal impairment are not available; it appears that no dosage adjustments are needed.
